Result: The results indicate that while LLMs can offer useful repair assistance, they are unreliable for high-risk real-world repair tasks without proper evaluation and safety safeguards. GPT-5.4 performs best overall, but all models show substantial errors, especially in phone repair, and Bangla responses are consistently worse than English ones.

Conclusion: The study concludes that LLMs have potential in consumer device repair but require rigorous evaluation and explicit safety safeguards to be reliable for high-risk tasks. There is a notable performance gap between English and Bangla responses, and phone repair stands out as the most difficult and safety-sensitive domain.

Abstract: Consumer device repair is an important but underexplored testbed for large language models (LLMs). Repair tasks require reasoning over incomplete problem descriptions, hardware-specific diagnostics, actionable troubleshooting, and safety-critical decisions, where incorrect advice can cause device damage, battery hazards, or permanent data loss. We introduce a benchmark of 991 real-world repair questions from Reddit spanning phone repair, computer repair, and data recovery, each paired with technician-written reference solutions, and provide Bangla translations to evaluate cross-lingual performance. We evaluate six state-of-the-art LLMs in English and Bangla using four repair-specific criteria: correctness, completeness, practicality, and safety. Our results show that while LLMs can provide useful repair assistance, they remain unreliable for high-risk real-world repair tasks without rigorous evaluation and explicit safety safeguards. Phone repair is the most difficult and safety-sensitive domain, and all models make substantial errors in board-level diagnosis, repair prioritization, and safe recovery procedures. Across domains and models, Bangla responses consistently perform worse than English responses. Among the evaluated models, GPT-5.4 performs best overall.

</details>


### [178] [Lingo_Research_Group at SemEval-2026 Task 9: Evaluating Prompt Variants for Polarization Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03334)
*Pritam Kadasi,Anuj Tiwari,Mayank Singh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents a submission for SemEval-2026 Task 9, focusing on multilingual text classification for polarization detection. It explores prompt-based approaches using two models across three subtasks, achieving varying degrees of success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation behind this paper is to address the challenge of multilingual text classification, specifically in the context of polarization detection. The authors aim to explore the effectiveness of prompt-based approaches in detecting polarization across different languages and subtasks.

Method: The authors adopt a systematic approach of research on short designed prompts, considering twelve different prompts. They conduct experiments using two models, aya-101 and Gemma3-27B, with the latter being chosen for the final submission. The system is evaluated on three subtasks: binary polarization detection, polarization type classification, and polarization manifestation identification.

Result: The system achieves an average macro level F1-score of 0.762 on Subtask 1, 0.587 on Subtask 2, and 0.444 on Subtask 3. The average accuracy is 0.819, 0.678, and 0.498, respectively. The results demonstrate that prompt-based approaches can be effective for coarse-grained polarization detection but encounter difficulties for fine-grained and multi-label sociolinguistic classification.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that prompt-based approaches can be used effectively for detecting coarse-grained polarization but face challenges in fine-grained and multi-label sociolinguistic classification. The authors highlight the difficulties in achieving high performance across 22 languages and three subtasks.

Abstract: Our submission presented in this paper is for SemEval-2026 Task 9: Multilingual Text Classification Challenge - Polarization Detection and it covers all three subtasks: (1) binary polarization detection, (2) polarization type classification and (3) polarization manifestation identification. We adopt a systematic approach of research on short designed prompts by considering twelve designed prompts that are different in terminology clarity, detail of the definition, guidance of reasoning and in-context examples use. The experiments are conducted using aya-101 and Gemma3-27B, with the latter chosen for the submission at the end of the development through performance considerations. Our system has an average macro level F1-score of 0.762 on Subtask 1, 0.587 on Subtask 2 and 0.444 on Subtask 3 with the average accuracy of 0.819, 0.678 and 0.498, respectively, on the official test set averaged among 22 languages, respectively. With cross-task and cross-lingual analysis, we demonstrate that prompt-based approaches can be used effectively to detect coarse grained polarization but encounter more and more difficulties as far as fine-grained and multi-label sociolinguistic classification is concerned.

</details>


### [179] [The Unsampled Truth: Psychometrics in SLMs Measure Prompt Artifacts, Not Psychological Constructs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03357)
*Nils Schwager,Christoph Hau,Simon Münker,Achim Rettinger*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Researchers test if large language models can accurately assess psychological traits, finding that model outputs are often influenced more by prompt artifacts than semantic understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate the premise that large language models' outputs reflect semantic reasoning when used for psychometric assessments.

Method: Using a prompt variation framework to separate semantic signals from prompt artifacts across 13 open-weight models with 0.6B to 14B parameters.

Result: Artifactual variance frequently overpowers the semantic signal, with models predominantly reflecting prompt compliance rather than simulated psychological traits.

Conclusion: The findings limit the utility of large language models in psychometrics, but the framework provides a diagnostic tool to identify destructive artifacts and isolate semantic understanding for future research.

Abstract: When prompting SLMs for psychometric assessments, researchers assume the outputs reflect semantic reasoning. We evaluate this premise across 13 open-weights models (0.6B to 14B parameters) using a prompt variation framework that separates semantic signals from prompt artifacts. By systematically varying personas, instructions, items, and option symbols, we find that artifactual variance frequently overpowers the semantic signal. In these cases, models predominantly reflect prompt compliance rather than simulated psychological traits. While these findings limit SLM utility in psychometrics, our framework provides a diagnostic tool to identify destructive artifacts and isolate semantic understanding for future frontier-model research.

</details>


### [180] [EntSQL: A Benchmark for Grounding Text-to-SQL in Long-Context Enterprise Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03363)
*Chengxi Liao,Tao Xu,Zulong Chen,Chuanfei Xu,Yiyan Wang,Xinyun Wang,Yanlong Zhang,Xiaojun Chen,Zhibo Yang,Zeyi Wen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduced EntSQL, a new Text-to-SQL benchmark focused on enterprise scenarios, highlighting the challenge of generating SQL with private business knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks overlook enterprise scenarios where SQL generation depends on private business knowledge, such as internal metrics and organizational rules.

Method: Introduced EntSQL, a benchmark with 1,066 aligned Chinese-English examples across five business domains, requiring domain knowledge and complex SQL structures.

Result: The best evaluated system achieved only 15.9% on English inputs when provided with long-form documents, indicating the difficulty of grounding SQL generation in enterprise knowledge.

Conclusion: EntSQL highlights the need for improved Text-to-SQL models that can incorporate private business knowledge and generate accurate SQL queries in enterprise scenarios.

Abstract: Text-to-SQL enables natural language access to databases, and recent LLMs have substantially advanced its capabilities. Existing benchmarks such as Spider, BIRD, and Spider~2.0 evaluate schema generalization, large-scale databases, and realistic workflows, but largely overlook enterprise scenarios where SQL generation depends on private business knowledge, such as internal metrics, reporting conventions, and organizational rules. We introduce EntSQL, an enterprise-oriented Text-to-SQL benchmark for evaluating long-context grounding over proprietary business documents. EntSQL contains 1,066 aligned Chinese-English semantic examples across five business domains, with most examples requiring domain knowledge beyond the question and schema and involving complex SQL structures. On English inputs, the best evaluated system reaches only 15.9\% when long-form documents are provided, highlighting the difficulty of grounding SQL generation in enterprise knowledge.

</details>


### [181] [See, Infer, Intervene: Proactive World Modeling for Goal-Oriented Social Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03371)
*Honghui Zhang,Chenmeinian Guo,Yichen Yu,Guanyu Liu,Yongming Qin,Chongguo Song,Mengyue Yang,Lei Yu,Tianyu Shi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a framework for proactive retail agents that can recognize customer behavior, infer their intent, and intervene appropriately. It introduces the Proactive Intent World Model (PIWM) and a benchmark called GuidanceSalesBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation behind this paper is to enable multimodal retail agents to proactively assist customers before they make an explicit request. The authors aim to create a system that can recognize customer behavior, understand their intent, and provide timely interventions.

Method: The authors propose the See--Infer--Intervene (SII) framework and instantiate it with the Proactive Intent World Model (PIWM). PIWM represents customer state using AIDA purchasing phases and BDI psychological fields, predicts action-conditioned intent transitions, and selects from five response classes.

Result: The results show that PIWM achieves a macro F1 score of 0.641 on held-out target videos, outperforming baselines. However, end-to-end video-only selection drops to 0.295, highlighting the challenge of video-to-state grounding. A preliminary real-store pilot achieves 0.579 action macro F1 on 20 fully annotated videos.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the proposed framework and model show promising results in proactive retail assistance. However, it also identifies video-to-state grounding as a significant deployment-time bottleneck that needs to be addressed.

Abstract: Multimodal retail agents should not only recognize what a customer is doing, but also decide whether and how to assist before an explicit request is made. We study this setting through the See--Infer--Intervene (SII) framework, where a device must see pre-interaction behavior, infer latent customer intent, and act by selecting an appropriate service intervention or choosing to wait. We instantiate SII with the Proactive Intent World Model (PIWM), which represents customer state with AIDA (Attention, Interest, Desire, Action) purchasing phases and BDI (belief, desire, intention) psychological fields, predicts action-conditioned intent transitions, and selects from five response classes: Greet, Elicit, Inform, Recommend, and Hold. We further construct GuidanceSalesBench, a smart-retail benchmark containing state manifests, pre-interaction videos, candidate responses, action-conditioned outcomes, and best-action labels. When conditioned on ground-truth customer state to isolate action selection, PIWM achieves 0.641 macro F1 on 30 held-out target videos, outperforming a zero-shot Qwen2.5-VL-7B baseline and training variants without balanced action supervision; end-to-end video-only selection drops to 0.295, below the 5-class balanced random baseline of 0.414, identifying video-to-state grounding as the dominant deployment-time bottleneck. A preliminary staged real-store pilot (recorded with paid participants performing scripted customer behaviors) reaches 0.579 action macro F1 on 20 fully annotated videos, with 10 additional accessible videos released with index-level labels.

</details>


### [182] [Causal Evidence of Stack Representations in Modeling Counter Languages Using Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03398)
*Nishit Singh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates the causal role of stack representations in transformer models, finding that they are necessary for model performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the inner mechanisms of transformers and the causal role of stack representations learned from next token prediction on counter languages.

Method: Training linear probes to predict stack depth from model hidden states and ablating the principal representation direction.

Result: Ablation of the stack representation direction causes sequential accuracy to collapse to near 0%, indicating its causal necessity for model performance.

Conclusion: The stack representation is not just learned but is causally necessary for model performance in transformers trained on next token prediction over counter languages.

Abstract: Formal languages have proven to be effective conduits to understand the inner mechanisms of transformers. Past work has shown that transformers trained on next token prediction over counter languages learn representations consistent with an underlying stack structure. Beyond representational analysis, this paper investigates the causal role of these representations. Linear probes are trained to predict the stack depth at each token from the model's hidden states, and a principal representation direction is extracted from the probe. Ablation of this direction from the model causes sequential accuracy to collapse to near 0%, providing strong empirical evidence that the stack representation is not just learned, but is causally necessary for model performance.

</details>


### [183] [Selective Token-Level Cryptographic Redaction for Privacy-Preserving Clinical Deployment of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03399)
*Farhan Sheth,Ziyuan Yang,Yongying Lan,Si Yong Yeo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A new framework called HERALD is proposed to balance privacy and usability in clinical applications of large language models by encrypting only sensitive tokens while preserving the surrounding context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to mitigate the risk of privacy leakage when using large language models for clinical applications, where sensitive health information is often sent to remote servers for processing.

Method: HERALD uses a token-level cryptographic redaction framework that combines medical named-entity recognizer (NER) with part-of-speech (POS) driven policies to select candidate tokens, and substitutes each protected token with a deterministic ciphertext.

Result: Experiments on public datasets show that HERALD consistently recovers performance close to plaintext, while maintaining the privacy of sensitive information.

Conclusion: HERALD provides a novel utilization pipeline that balances privacy and usability in clinical applications of large language models, and can be used in a model-agnostic manner on the client side.

Abstract: While large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for clinical applications, many existing pipelines require sending raw sensitive health information to remote servers for processing, which heightens the risk of privacy leakage. A natural approach to mitigate this risk is to encrypt the data before transmission. However, straightforward solutions such as encrypting the entire dataset introduce prohibitive computational, alignment, and communication overheads, rendering large-scale practical deployment infeasible. To preserve privacy while maintaining usability, we present Healthcare Encryption & Redaction via Adaptive Linguistic Decomposition (HERALD), a token-level cryptographic redaction framework designed to achieve this balance by encrypting only sensitive tokens while preserving the surrounding context for downstream model utility. HERALD combines medical named-entity recognizer (NER) with part-of-speech (POS) driven policies to select candidate tokens, performs targeted lemmatization to stabilize surface forms, and substitutes each protected token with a deterministic ciphertext wrapped in explicit delimiters. Notably, HERALD is model-agnostic and operates entirely on the client side, ensuring that sensitive content remains encrypted throughout storage, transmission, and processing without requiring changes to downstream models. We evaluated HERALD on both classification and medical question answering (MQA) tasks on public datasets. Across different tasks, experiments illustrate that fully secured baselines suffer significant utility loss, whereas HERALD consistently recovers performance close to plaintext. Overall, HERALD provides a novel utilization pipeline.

</details>


### [184] [Lexicons and grammars for language processing: industrial or handcrafted products?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03412)
*Eric Laporte*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The use of linguistic data for language processing has increased, with language resources including texts, electronic lexicons, and formal grammars. The construction of lexicons and grammars is mostly manual, while corpora are highly automated. A tension exists between manual construction and automated generation of these resources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation behind this paper is to analyze the current state of language resources, specifically the construction of lexicons, grammars, and corpora, and to discuss the future directions of these resources, whether manual construction, automated generation, or a combination of both.

Method: The paper analyzes practical examples of language resources and discusses the pros and cons of manual construction versus automated generation.

Result: The paper identifies two trends in the construction of language resources: manual construction by linguists and automated generation by computer engineers. It highlights the tension between these two approaches and suggests that the future of language resources depends on which trend will yield the best results.

Conclusion: The conclusion of this paper is that the future of language resources depends on finding the best approach to constructing lexicons, grammars, and corpora, whether through manual construction, automated generation, or a combination of both.

Abstract: During the recent years, the use of linguistic data for language processing increased progressively. Such data are now commonly called language resources. Most of the language resources used for this purpose are collections of texts as the Brown Corpus and the Penn Treebank, but electronic lexicons (WordNet, FrameNet, VerbNet, ComLex, Lexicon-Grammar...) and formal grammars (TAG...) developed recently. Most processes of construction of lexicons and grammars are manual, whereas the construction of corpora has always been highly automated. However, more and more specialists of language processing realize that the information content of lexicons and grammars is richer than that of corpora, and hence the former make more elaborate processing possible. The difference in construction time is likely to be connected with the difference in information content: the handcrafting of lexicons and grammars by linguists would make them more informative than automatically generated data. This situation can evolve into two directions: either specialists of language technology get progressively used to handling manually constructed resources, which are more informative and more complex, or the process of construction of lexicons and grammars is automated and industrialized, which is the mainstream perspective. Both evolutions are already in progress, and a tension exists between them. The relation between linguists and computer scientists depends on the future of these evolutions, since the first implies training and hiring numerous linguists, whereas the other depends essentially on solutions elaborated by computer engineers. The aim of this article is to analyse practical examples of the language resources in question, and to discuss about which of the two trends, handcrafting or generating industrially, or a combination of both, can give the best results or is the most realistic.

</details>


### [185] [Large Language Models Are Overconfident in Their Own Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03437)
*Mario Sanz-Guerrero,Manuel Mager,Katharina von der Wense*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The study investigates how chat templates affect the calibration of conversational large language models (LLMs), finding that the template causes an 'ownership bias' leading to overconfidence in model responses, and proposes a simple strategy to improve calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation behind this paper is to understand the impact of chat templates on the calibration of conversational LLMs, specifically addressing why instruction-tuned LLMs are less well-calibrated than their base pre-trained counterparts.

Method: The study decouples the effects of post-training algorithms and chat formats on LLM calibration, conducting experiments across six recent open-weight LLMs, three benchmarks, and three confidence elicitation methods.

Result: The results show that models assign up to 26% higher confidence to their own responses due to an 'ownership bias', and that framing the model's answer as user input during confidence elicitation can significantly reduce overconfidence and improve calibration by up to 26%.

Conclusion: The study concludes that chat templates can aggravate the miscalibration of conversational LLMs through an 'ownership bias', but that this issue can be addressed with a simple inference-time strategy, narrowing the gap between base and instruction-tuned models.

Abstract: Prior work has shown that instruction-tuned large language models (LLMs) are less well calibrated than their base pre-trained counterparts. However, little is known about the frequently used chat template's effect on the calibration of conversational LLMs. In this work, we investigate the mechanisms driving this miscalibration by decoupling the effects of the post-training algorithm and the chat format. We find that, while instruction tuning fundamentally harms calibration, the chat template aggravates the issue through an "ownership bias" -- models are significantly more confident in their own answers than in identical answers provided by a user. Extensive experiments across six recent open-weight LLMs, three benchmarks, and three confidence elicitation methods show that models assign up to 26% higher confidence to their own responses. Leveraging this insight, we propose a simple inference-time strategy: framing the model's answer as user input during confidence elicitation. This approach significantly reduces overconfidence and improves calibration by up to 26% without the need for retraining, narrowing the gap between base and instruction-tuned models.

</details>


### [186] [BaltiVoice: A Speech Corpus and Fine-tuned Whisper ASR System for the Balti Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03504)
*Muhammad Ali*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A read-speech corpus for Balti language is presented, along with a fine-tuned ASR model achieving 30.07% WER.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide ASR resources for Balti, a Tibetic language with no prior publicly available resources.

Method: Fine-tuning OpenAI Whisper-small on a 16.8-hour Balti read-speech corpus.

Result: A Word Error Rate (WER) of 30.07% on a held-out validation set, improved from a zero-shot baseline of 182.18%.

Conclusion: The dataset, fine-tuned model, and live transcription demo are publicly available, facilitating Balti language ASR development.

Abstract: We present BaltiVoice, a 16.8-hour read-speech corpus for Balti (ISO 639-3: bft), a Tibetic language spoken in Gilgit-Baltistan, Pakistan, with no prior publicly available ASR resources. The corpus contains 10,060 validated utterances in native Nastaliq script, derived from Mozilla Common Voice recordings. We fine-tune OpenAI Whisper-small on this corpus and report a Word Error Rate (WER) of 30.07% on a held-out validation set of 538 utterances, down from a measured zero-shot baseline of 182.18% for Whisper-small on Balti. The dataset, fine-tuned model, and a live transcription demo are publicly available on HuggingFace.

</details>


### [187] [AutoTail-BSFGM: Class-Balance-Aware Fine-Tuning for Chinese Scholarly Text Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03576)
*Anling Xiang,Yuwen Yang,Yang Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A new method, AutoTail-BSFGM, is proposed to improve class-balance-aware fine-tuning for Chinese scholarly text classification, achieving consistent gains in abstract-based scholarly classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chinese scholarly corpora often contain imbalanced and semantically adjacent disciplinary labels, making class-balance-aware fine-tuning necessary for improving literature organization and research intelligence.

Method: AutoTail-BSFGM combines an automatically gated tail-prior adjustment, a weak Balanced Softmax auxiliary loss, and Fast Gradient Method adversarial regularization to improve class-balance-aware fine-tuning.

Result: AutoTail-BSFGM improves validation and lockbox accuracy on two CSL-based tasks, with MacBERT-base achieving increases of 0.83 and 0.49 points, respectively, and shows consistent gains in abstract-based scholarly classification.

Conclusion: The proposed method, AutoTail-BSFGM, effectively improves class-balance-sensitive behavior and yields consistent gains for abstract-based scholarly classification, supporting its potential application in literature organization and research intelligence.

Abstract: Scholarly text classification supports literature organization, subject indexing, and research intelligence, but Chinese scholarly corpora often contain imbalanced and semantically adjacent disciplinary labels. We propose AutoTail-BSFGM, a class-balance-aware fine-tuning method that combines an automatically gated tail-prior adjustment, a weak Balanced Softmax auxiliary loss, and Fast Gradient Method adversarial regularization. The method changes only the training objective and procedure; inference uses the same single base-size encoder and linear classifier as the corresponding label-smoothed baseline. We evaluate the method on two CSL-based tasks: an abstract-to-discipline task with 67 labels and a title-to-category task with 13 categories. On the primary abstract task, AutoTail-BSFGM improves validation and lockbox accuracy under both Chinese RoBERTa-WWM and MacBERT-base. With MacBERT-base, validation accuracy increases by 0.83 percentage points and lockbox accuracy by 0.49 points, with a pooled paired McNemar signal on validation (p = 0.023). On the title task, the method improves validation accuracy by 0.70 points and validation balanced accuracy by 2.64 points; lockbox accuracy is approximately neutral while lockbox balanced accuracy improves by 1.22 points. The results support a bounded contribution: AutoTail-BSFGM improves class-balance-sensitive behavior and yields consistent gains for abstract-based scholarly classification, without uniformly improving every metric on every split.

</details>


### [188] [Beyond the Literal: Decomposing Pragmatic Intent in Multimodal Meme Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03604)
*Zhengyi Zhao,Shubo Zhang,Zezhong Wang,Luyao Ye,Huimin Wang,Hanqi Yan,Binyang Li,Kam-Fai Wong,Yulan He*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The authors propose Intent Projection, a framework to improve Large Vision Language Models' (LVLMs) understanding of memes and sarcastic posts by separating literal and pragmatic meanings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LVLMs struggle to understand memes and sarcastic posts, often describing the image rather than the author's intended meaning.

Method: The authors propose a framework called Intent Projection, which separates literal and pragmatic signals at the representation, output, and objective levels within a single LVLM backbone.

Result: Intent Projection consistently outperforms open-source baselines and narrows the gap to proprietary models across six multimodal benchmarks, with the largest gains on high-divergence posts.

Conclusion: The proposed Intent Projection framework effectively improves LVLMs' understanding of memes and sarcastic posts by decomposing literal and pragmatic meanings.

Abstract: When asked what a meme or sarcastic post means, Large Vision Language Models (LVLMs) tend to describe what the image shows rather than what the author is trying to communicate. Standard instruction tuning entangles a post's literal content with its pragmatic meaning, letting surface-level details contaminate the final response. We reframe meme understanding as a problem of literal-pragmatic decomposition and propose \textbf{Intent Projection}, a framework that separates the two signals at the representation, output, and objective levels within a single LVLM backbone. At the representation level, an orthogonal projection module removes dominant unimodal directions from the fused image-text representation, retaining only the pragmatic residual, while a surface-real affect classifier anchors the decoder with a discrete tag that names the polarity gap. At the output level, the model externalizes a structured reasoning chain, and at the objective level a contrastive reward explicitly penalizes answers that restate the literal description. Across six multimodal benchmarks, Intent Projection consistently outperforms open-source baselines and narrows the gap to proprietary models, with the largest gains on high-divergence posts where literal collapse is most damaging.

</details>


### [189] [Building Reliable Long-Form Generation via Hallucination Rejection Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03628)
*Lin Li,Georgia Channing,Suhaas M Bhat,Gabriel Davis Jones,Yarin Gal*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a framework called Segment-wise HAllucination Rejection Sampling (SHARS) to mitigate hallucinations in large language models during long-form text generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are prone to hallucinating incorrect content, which worsens in long-form generation due to 'hallucination snowballing', where early errors compound into subsequent outputs.

Method: The SHARS framework uses an arbitrary hallucination detector to identify and reject hallucinated segments during generation and resample until faithful content is produced. It adopts semantic uncertainty as the detector with vital modifications for long-form text.

Result: Empirical evaluations show that the method substantially reduces hallucinations in long-form generation while preserving or improving the informativeness of generation.

Conclusion: The proposed SHARS framework effectively mitigates hallucinations in large language models during long-form text generation without requiring external resources, enhancing factual consistency and reliability.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable progress in open-ended text generation, yet they remain prone to hallucinating incorrect or unsupported content, which undermines their reliability. This issue is exacerbated in long-form generation due to hallucination snowballing, a phenomenon where early errors propagate and compound into subsequent outputs. To address this challenge, we propose a novel inference-time hallucination mitigation framework, named Segment-wise HAllucination Rejection Sampling (SHARS), which uses an arbitrary hallucination detector to identify and reject hallucinated segments during generation and resample until faithful content is produced. By retaining only confident information and building subsequent generations upon it, the framework mitigates hallucination accumulation and enhances factual consistency. To instantiate this framework, we adopt semantic uncertainty as the detector and introduce several vital modifications to address its limitations and better adapt it to long-form text. Our method enables models to self-correct hallucinations without requiring external resources such as web search or knowledge bases, while remaining compatible with them for future extensions. Empirical evaluations on standardized hallucination benchmarks demonstrate that our method substantially reduces hallucinations in long-form generation while preserving or even improving the informativeness of generation. Code is available at: https://github.com/TreeLLi/hallucination-rejection-sampling.

</details>


### [190] [Safety Measurements for Fine-tuned LLMs Should be Grounded in Capability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03648)
*Krishnapriya Vishnubhotla,Hillary Dawkins,Isar Nejadgholi,Svetlana Kiritchenko*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Fine-tuning large language models can compromise safety, and a multi-dimensional evaluation is needed to understand its effects on both capability and safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous works on fine-tuning large language models have limited and inconsistent experimental settings, making it difficult to draw meaningful conclusions about safety impacts.

Method: A multi-dimensional evaluation of fine-tuning effects on model behavior, focusing on both capability and safety, with a specific capability goal.

Result: Fine-tuned models can produce incoherent generations in response to safety prompts, automated safety judgments are unreliable for such outputs, and conclusions about fine-tuning effects can change depending on safety benchmarks and evaluators.

Conclusion: Anchoring fine-tuning to a specific capability goal is essential for evaluating safety impacts and comparing mitigation methods, highlighting the need for a consistent and multi-dimensional approach.

Abstract: Adapting foundation large language models to a user's task or preferred style through fine-tuning can result in compromising the model's safety. Previous works examined the effects of fine-tuning on model safety in limited and seemingly random experimental settings. We argue that anchoring fine-tuning to a specific capability goal is essential for avoiding arbitrary empirical choices, allowing us to draw meaningful conclusions about safety impacts, and to compare mitigation methods on a consistent basis. We conduct a multi-dimensional evaluation of the effects of fine-tuning on model behavior by focusing on capability as well as safety. Our results surface important issues that (1) fine-tuned models can produce incoherent generations in response to safety prompts, (2) automated safety judgments are unreliable for such incoherent outputs, and (3) the conclusions about the effects of fine-tuning can change depending on the choice of safety benchmark as well as the safety evaluator.

</details>


### [191] [CoEval: Ranking Language Models for Custom Tasks Without Labeled Data or Trustworthy Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03650)
*Alexander Apartsin,Yehudit Aperstein*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents CoEval, a framework for evaluating language models without task-specific labeled data, generating a benchmark and ranking models with a cross-family judge ensemble.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The difficulty in choosing or ranking language models for a specific application when no task-specific labeled data exists and standard benchmarks may be contaminated with pretraining data.

Method: CoEval uses teacher models to synthesize a fresh benchmark with attribute control, and a cross-family judge ensemble to rank candidate models without human raters.

Result: CoEval recovers the true model ranking, tracks ground-truth correctness with ho=0.86, and shows zero verbatim overlap with major public benchmarks.

Conclusion: CoEval provides a label-free, contamination-free leaderboard that can be regenerated for any application, allowing teams to evaluate language models without relying on human labels or contaminated benchmarks.

Abstract: Choosing or ranking language models for a specific application is hardest when no task-specific labeled data exists, and standard public benchmarks cannot be trusted, their items having likely leaked into pretraining, so scores reflect memorization rather than fitness. We present CoEval, an open-source, reusable framework that closes this gap end to end: from only a description of a task or domain, teacher models synthesize a fresh, attribute-controlled benchmark with no human labels, contamination-free because items are generated anew on each run, and a cross-family judge ensemble ranks candidate models with no human raters. Validated where ground truth exists, CoEval recovers the true model ranking and tracks ground-truth correctness at ho=0.86. The label-free judging needs no human calibration because judge-panel composition (vendor diversity), not size, drives reliability: a small, well-chosen cross-family panel is most reliable, while a single judge can be anti-correlated with ground truth (judge-choice regret 0.35) and the ensemble never is. Generated items show zero verbatim 13-gram overlap with five major public benchmarks; the panel cancels verbosity bias and precludes same-family self-preference. A four-task study produced 7,978 evaluations for USD 5.89. The same declarative pipeline applies to any domain and is cheap enough to re-run on every model release: a label-free, contamination-free leaderboard any team can regenerate for its own application.

</details>


### [192] [Does Language Shift Break Medical Vision-Language Models? Indonesian Radiology Visual Question Answering Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03693)
*Pieter Christy Yan Yudhistira,Dzaki Rafif Malik,Novanto Yudistira*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces IndoRad-VQA, a dataset for evaluating medical vision-language models in Indonesian, and finds that models performing well on English benchmarks may not be robust in non-English clinical contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess the robustness of medical vision-language models under non-English clinical language, specifically in Indonesian.

Method: The authors translated radiology question-answer pairs into Indonesian with quality control, and evaluated general-purpose, Southeast Asian multilingual, and medical-specific VLMs under English and Indonesian prompting settings.

Result: The models showed a performance gap of 8 to 25 percent between English and Indonesian settings, indicating that strong performance on English medical VQA benchmarks does not necessarily translate to robust behavior in Indonesian clinical contexts.

Conclusion: The study highlights the need for more inclusive multilingual evaluation of medical multimodal foundation models, and makes the IndoRad-VQA dataset available for further research.

Abstract: Medical Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are typically evaluated on English radiology visual question answering benchmarks, leaving their robustness under non-English clinical language largely unexplored. We introduce IndoRad-VQA, an Indonesian adaptation of VQA-RAD, to assess whether medical VLMs retain radiology reasoning ability when questions are asked in Bahasa Indonesia. Radiology question-answer pairs are translated into Indonesian with self-evaluation-based quality control to preserve clinical meaning, terminology consistency, and answer equivalence. We evaluate general-purpose, Southeast Asian multilingual, and medical-specific VLMs under English and Indonesian prompting settings. Beyond accuracy, we quantify the language robustness gap between English and Indonesian inputs. We also conduct an error analysis to identify failure modes of question answering, such as yes/no flips, laterality errors, and output-language mismatches. Our findings show that strong performance on English medical VQA benchmarks does not necessarily translate to robust behavior in Indonesian clinical contexts. We observe a performance gap of 8 to 25 percent between the English and Indonesian settings, depending on the evaluation metric. These results highlight the need for more inclusive multilingual evaluation of medical multimodal foundation models. The dataset is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/Lab-IS/IndoRad-VQA.

</details>


### [193] [Don't Forget Your Embeddings: Robust Knowledge Erasure via Precise Editing of Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03695)
*Clara Haya Suslik,Or Shafran,Mor Geva*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces EMBER, a module for erasing specific knowledge from language models by targeting the embedding layer, which improves erasure efficacy and robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for erasing knowledge from language models have limitations, as the target knowledge can often be recovered through adversarial prompting or relearning.

Method: The authors propose EMBedding ERasure (EMBER), a plug-n-play module that uses Sparse Matrix Factorization to erase concept-related features from token embeddings.

Result: EMBER consistently improves erasure efficacy and specificity across task formats, with minimal coherence loss, and reduces regained accuracy by up to 50% when combined with existing methods.

Conclusion: The paper establishes that precise embedding-level intervention is necessary for robust concept erasure and demonstrates that existing methods can benefit from such augmentation.

Abstract: As language models are increasingly deployed in real-world applications, the ability to erase specific knowledge from them becomes critical for safety and compliance. Prominent methods seek persistent removal by updating the model's parameters, yet the target knowledge often can be recovered through adversarial prompting or relearning. In this work, we hypothesize this limitation stems in part from existing methods overlooking the embedding layer. To address this, we introduce EMBedding ERasure (EMBER), a plug-n-play erasure module that leverages Sparse Matrix Factorization for precise erasure of concept-related features from token embeddings. Through comprehensive evaluations across diverse concepts on Gemma-2-2B-it and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, we find that augmenting existing methods with EMBER consistently improves erasure efficacy and specificity across task formats, with minimal coherence loss. Moreover, it dramatically improves robustness to relearning, reducing regained accuracy by up to 50%, limiting it to 35% on Llama compared to 70%-76% for prior methods. Further analysis shows that the coherence cost is localized, affecting only a small set of concept-exclusive tokens. Our work establishes that precise embedding-level intervention is necessary for robust concept erasure, and demonstrates that existing methods can benefit from such augmentation.

</details>


### [194] [Re-Ranking Through an Attribution Lens for Citation Quality in Legal QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03728)
*Mohamed Hesham Elganayni,Selim Saleh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a method to improve legal question answering by re-ranking passages using a lightweight cross-encoder trained on perturbation-based attribution scores, which leads to better citation faithfulness and alignment with expert answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the limitation that semantic similarity does not correlate with passage attribution in retrieval-augmented generation systems for legal question answering.

Method: The method involves training a lightweight cross-encoder on continuous perturbation-based attribution scores to re-rank passages prior to generation.

Result: The re-ranker substantially improves citation faithfulness and alignment with gold expert answers, and two re-rankers trained independently on different models converge beyond their raw attribution agreement.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that perturbation-based attribution provides a practical, model-agnostic training signal for citation-aware retrieval, and the proposed approach can improve the performance of retrieval-augmented generation systems for legal question answering.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation systems for legal question answering typically retrieve passages based on semantic similarity and provide them to a language model, which then generates cited answers. Prior work assumes that highly ranked passages are most likely to be usefully cited by the model. Perturbation-based attribution methods, such as C-LIME, have been used exclusively for post-hoc explanation. However, on the AQuAECHR benchmark, semantic similarity does not correlate with passage attribution. Within a retriever's candidate pool, similarity-based ranking performs worse than random selection at surfacing gold citation paragraphs. To address this limitation, a lightweight cross-encoder is trained on continuous perturbation-based attribution scores to re-rank passages prior to generation. This approach is evaluated on the AQuAECHR benchmark, using two language models and five-fold cross-validation. The re-ranker substantially improves citation faithfulness and alignment with gold expert answers. Notably, two re-rankers trained independently on different models converge beyond their raw attribution agreement. This finding indicates that the cross-encoder reduces model-specific noise and produces a shared relevance signal that partially transfers across models, although same-model re-ranking remains more effective. These results demonstrate that perturbation-based attribution provides a practical, model-agnostic training signal for citation-aware retrieval.

</details>


### [195] [Entropy Gate: Entropy Quenching for Near-Lossless Token Compression in LLM Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03739)
*Justice Owusu Agyemang,Jerry John Kponyo,Kwame Opuni-Boachie Obour Agyekum,Francisca Adoma Acheampong,Kwame Agyeman-Prempeh Agyekum,James Dzisi Gadze*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduced Entropy Gate, a token compression framework that reduces token usage in LLM pipelines while preserving semantic fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM pipelines waste tokens on low-information content, and the authors aim to reduce this waste.

Method: The Entropy Gate framework uses entropy quenching to progressively remove low-energy tokens, with an adaptive quenching schedule and a fidelity gate to preserve semantic fidelity.

Result: The framework achieves 40-60% compression across five prompt categories while maintaining semantic fidelity, and up to 96% reduction in token usage when combined with external memory.

Conclusion: The Entropy Gate framework offers a stateless, model-agnostic solution for reducing token usage in LLM pipelines while preserving semantic fidelity.

Abstract: LLM pipelines waste substantial token budgets on low-information content: repeated context, verbose responses, and redundant boilerplate. We introduce Entropy Gate, a token compression framework applying entropy quenching $-$ a thermodynamic process that progressively freezes out low-energy tokens while preserving semantic fidelity. Each token receives a multi-factor information energy $E(t)$ combining statistical, structural, and positional components. An adaptive quenching schedule $T(τ) = T_0 / (1 + ατ)$ removes tokens whose Boltzmann survival probability $p_i = \exp(-E_i / kT)$ falls below threshold, with a fidelity gate halting compression when energy-weighted similarity drops below $θ$. We prove token selection by descending $E(t)$ maximizes expected semantic preservation, that quenching produces nested survival sets, and that achievable compression approaches the information-theoretic limit $\text{CR} \to 1 - I(P; T)/H(P)$. A Phase 1 heuristic achieves 40-60% compression across five prompt categories while maintaining $S_E > 0.80$, with energy-squared amplification $E \to E^2$ adding 10-25 percentage points. Context deduplication adds 50-70% savings on repeated blocks. Output-side quenching, motivated by findings that brevity improves accuracy, further reduces response overhead. Combined with external memory, reduction composes multiplicatively to 88-96% for agentic workloads. The framework is stateless, model-agnostic, and deploys as an OpenAI-compatible HTTP proxy.

</details>


### [196] [Framing Migration News with LLMs: Structured CoT as a Support for Human Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03761)
*David Alonso del Barrio,Jing Wen,Daniel Gatica-Perez*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper explores using a locally deployable open-source LLM to support interpretable frame analysis of migration news, introducing a Structured Chain-of-Thought (SCoT) prompting approach to improve transparency and auditability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need for accurate, transparent, auditable, and accessible tools for media scholars and researchers to study migration narratives, due to concerns about data privacy, reproducibility, and equitable access with existing LLM-based approaches.

Method: The authors propose a SCoT prompting approach using Llama3-8B, enabling step-by-step justifications grounded in predefined framing categories, and evaluate it on a dataset of migration-related news.

Result: The SCoT approach improves classification performance over baselines, and human-centered evaluation shows that SCoT explanations are perceived as logical and can prompt reflection on initial interpretations.

Conclusion: The work highlights the potential and risks of LLM-assisted frame analysis, contributing to discussions on responsible and accessible computational tools for studying socially impactful media narratives.

Abstract: Frame analysis of migration news is a socially consequential task: media scholars and researchers who study how migration is narrated need tools that are not only accurate, but transparent, auditable, and accessible within the resource constraints typical of academic research groups. Existing LLM-based approaches rely on proprietary APIs and large models that raise concerns about data privacy, reproducibility and equitable access among media researchers. This work studies how a locally deployable open-source LLM can support interpretable frame analysis as an assistive tool. We introduce a Structured Chain-of-Thought (SCoT) prompting approach using Llama3-8B, enabling step-by-step justifications grounded in predefined framing categories. This structured design allows users to audit model outputs and examine alternative interpretations in a task that is inherently subjective. We evaluate our approach on a dataset of migration-related news and show that SCoT improves classification performance over zero-shot and few-shot baselines while remaining feasible on a single GPU. Then, we conduct a human-centered evaluation in which annotators assess the coherence and influence of "the model's reasoning". Results indicate that SCoT explanations are generally perceived as logical (mean score 4.1/5, though with notable variation across texts) and can prompt reflection on initial interpretations, even when disagreement persists. Our findings highlight both the potential and risks of LLM-assisted frame analysis. While structured reasoning can increase the traceability of model outputs and support critical interpretation, it can also influence human judgment in subtle ways. By enabling local deployment and emphasizing human-in-the-loop interaction, this work contributes to discussions on responsible and accessible computational tools for the study of socially impactful media narratives.

</details>


### [197] [HybridThinker: Efficient Chain-of-Thought Reasoning via Compressed Memory and Transient Thought Steps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03768)
*Xin Liu,Runsong Zhao,Xinyu Liu,Junhao Ruan,Pengcheng Huang,Shichao Dong,Chunyang Xiao,Chenglong Wang,Changliang Li,Jingbo Zhu,Tong Xiao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A new method called HybridThinker is proposed to improve the efficiency of chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning in large language models (LLMs) by temporarily retaining fine-grained thought steps while compressing them into compact representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CoT compression methods lose fine-grained information, making subsequent steps more error-prone, and the proposed method aims to alleviate this issue.

Method: HybridThinker combines preserving compact representations of thought steps with temporarily retaining fine-grained details, and uses a hybrid training scheme to ensure the model effectively uses compressed memory tokens.

Result: HybridThinker matches the uncompressed baseline and achieves a 5.8 point average accuracy gain over existing CoT compression methods on 4 reasoning benchmarks, with similar inference time.

Conclusion: The proposed HybridThinker method effectively balances the trade-off between accuracy and efficiency in CoT reasoning, and ablation studies confirm the importance of both temporary thought-step retention and the hybrid training scheme.

Abstract: Extended chain-of-thought (CoT) traces improve LLM reasoning but incur substantial computational and memory costs. While existing CoT compression methods mitigate this by condensing thought steps into compact representations via memory tokens and retaining only these representations at inference time, the loss of fine-grained information makes subsequent steps more error-prone. To alleviate this, we propose \textbf{HybridThinker}, where in addition to preserved these representations, thought steps are also temporarily retained to provide fine-grained details. However, we observe that naively keeping thought steps accessible to subsequent steps \emph{during training} lets the model bypass memory tokens by retrieving information directly from these steps, leaving the model's ability to compress and retrieve information through memory tokens insufficiently trained. We therefore introduce a hybrid training scheme, in which only some thought steps are directly accessible through attention to subsequent steps, while the other thought steps are masked, forcing the model to use memory tokens for compression and retrieval. Across 4 reasoning benchmarks, HybridThinker matches the uncompressed baseline, advancing the state of the art in CoT compression by 5.8 points on average accuracy with similar inference time. Ablation studies confirm that both temporary thought-step retention and the hybrid training scheme contribute to these gains.

</details>


### [198] [KletterMix: Climbing Toward High-Quality German Pretraining Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03773)
*Maurice Kraus,Ruben Härle,Sebastian Sztwiertnia,Abbas Goher Khan,Mehdi Ali,Michael Fromm,Kristian Kersting*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A high-quality German corpus called KletterMix is introduced for language model pretraining, created by translating a state-of-the-art English corpus while preserving key features, and shown to improve German-language model performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the lack of high-quality German-language resources for language model pretraining, which are often smaller, less curated, and less validated than their English counterparts.

Method: KletterMix is built by translating a state-of-the-art English pretraining corpus into German, preserving document boundaries, metadata, source structure, and topical diversity, and then documenting the dataset through various corpus-level analyses.

Result: Models trained on KletterMix achieve measurable improvements on German-language downstream evaluations compared to established German corpora, demonstrating that carefully curated translated data can strengthen the German pretraining data ecosystem.

Conclusion: The introduction of KletterMix, a high-quality German corpus for language model pretraining, helps to bridge the gap between German and English language model resources, and shows that careful translation and curation can lead to improved model performance.

Abstract: High-quality pretraining data is a central ingredient in modern language models, but German-language resources remain far less developed than their English counterparts: they are often smaller, less carefully curated, weakly documented, and rarely validated through controlled training experiments. We introduce KletterMix, a high-quality German corpus for language model pretraining and annealing, designed as a reusable dataset artifact for the natural language processing and modeling community. KletterMix is built by translating a state-of-the-art English pretraining corpus into German while preserving document boundaries, metadata, source structure, and topical diversity. This construction yields a German corpus with the scale and diversity of a modern pretraining dataset, while enabling direct comparison to its English source. We document the dataset through a broad set of corpus-level analyses, including translation quality, document length distributions, topic coverage, source composition, and geographic metadata. Using COMETKiwi, we show that the translated documents achieve strong quality across diverse domains, suggesting that careful translation can preserve much of the semantic and stylistic richness of the original corpus. Beyond dataset construction, we evaluate KletterMix as training data. Through controlled pretraining and annealing ablations against established German corpora, we show that models trained on KletterMix achieve measurable improvements on German-language downstream evaluations. These results demonstrate that carefully curated translated data can substantially strengthen the German pretraining data ecosystem.

</details>


### [199] [Expert-Aware Causal Tracing of Factual Recall in Sparse MoE Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03780)
*Yuetian Lu,Ali Modarressi,Yihong Liu,Hinrich Schütze*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies causal tracing of factual recall in sparse mixture-of-experts (MoE) language models, focusing on identifying which expert contributions matter when a factual prediction is mediated by a routed MoE block.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study aims to understand how factual recall is mediated in sparse MoE language models, which introduce a new challenge in causal tracing due to their routed expert structure.

Method: The authors propose expert-aware causal tracing for sparse MoE language models, using interventions such as adding noise to subject-token embeddings and testing the restoration of true-vs-foil logit contrast with clean MoE-block outputs or expert-level updates.

Result: The results show that MoE factual tracing can be made expert-aware, but expert-level localization is model- and protocol-dependent. For Qwen3-30B-A3B-Base, a specific expert (L44E069) is identified as crucial, while for Mixtral-8x7B-v0.1, the signal is not localized to a single expert but is recovered with routed multi-expert updates.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates the feasibility of expert-aware causal tracing in sparse MoE language models, highlighting the complexity and model-specific nature of expert contributions to factual recall.

Abstract: Causal tracing of factual recall has been studied predominantly in dense transformer language models, where interventions localize information flow to layers or feed-forward modules. Sparse mixture-of-experts (MoE) language models introduce a sharper question: when a factual prediction is mediated by a routed MoE block, which routed expert contributions matter? We formulate expert-aware causal tracing for sparse MoE language models. Using CounterFact facts, we first corrupt the model's factual preference by adding noise to subject-token embeddings, and then test whether clean MoE-block outputs or clean expert-level updates restore the true-vs-foil logit contrast. For Qwen3-30B-A3B-Base, a layer sweep selects and validates layer 44, and expert-level tracing identifies L44E069 as an expert repeatedly selected in the clean run whose held-out patch outperforms other active same-layer expert patches. For Mixtral-8x7B-v0.1, layer-level tracing validates a mid-layer signal, but the signal is not localized to the selected singleton expert; a coalition check instead recovers it with routed multi-expert updates. These results suggest that MoE factual tracing can be made expert-aware, while also showing that expert-level localization is model- and protocol-dependent rather than universal.

</details>


### [200] [Reasoning over Grammar: Can Synthetic Linguistic Reasoning Traces Enhance Low-Resource Machine Translation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03782)
*Renhao Pei,Yihong Liu,Sampo Pyysalo,Hinrich Schütze,Shaoxiong Ji*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper explores using structured linguistic analysis and grammatical reasoning to improve machine translation for low-resource languages using large language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models struggle to effectively apply grammatical information during translation, particularly for low-resource languages.

Method: The authors propose a pipeline to automatically generate step-by-step linguistic reasoning traces and evaluate them in three settings: in-context learning (ICL), supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT).

Result: Linguistic reasoning traces are most effective as inference-time guidance in ICL, improving translation performance across most models, languages, and metrics.

Conclusion: LLMs can leverage grammatical information for low-resource MT when given reliable linguistic analyses, but learning to generate such analyses remains a major bottleneck.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) offer a promising approach to machine translation (MT) for extremely low-resource languages by incorporating linguistic resources through in-context learning. However, LLMs often struggle to apply grammatical information effectively during translation. Inspired by recent progress in chain-of-thought reasoning, we investigate whether low-resource MT can benefit from structured intermediate steps of linguistic analysis and grammatical reasoning. We propose a pipeline for automatically generating step-by-step linguistic reasoning traces from Universal Dependencies treebanks, dictionaries, and grammar-rule banks. We evaluate these traces in three settings: in-context learning (ICL), supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT), on Xibe and Chintang as test cases. Our results show that linguistic reasoning traces are most effective as inference-time guidance: in ICL, reliable sentence-specific traces substantially improve translation performance across most models, languages, and metrics. In contrast, using the linguistic reasoning traces as training data yields smaller and less consistent gains, as models learn the trace format but often generate erroneous content. These findings suggest that LLMs can leverage grammatical information for low-resource MT when given reliable linguistic analyses, while learning to generate such analyses remains a major bottleneck.

</details>


### [201] [Backdoor Unlearning Generalization: A Path Toward the Removal of Unknown Triggers in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03785)
*Lisa Bouger,Théo Lasnier,Philippe Looubet Moundi,Yannick Teglia,Djamé Seddah*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The study explores backdoor neutralization through unlearning in Large Language Models (LLMs), finding that removing one backdoor can suppress others, even if unknown.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing defenses against backdoor attacks in LLMs are limited as they target one backdoor at a time and require knowledge of the trigger, putting defenders at a disadvantage.

Method: The study analyzes three model families with backdoors injected via pretraining or continual pretraining, using a new metric called Cross Activation Shift Distance to quantify model changes.

Result: The results show that unlearning certain backdoors can induce the suppression of others, suggesting a new direction for LLM safety through controlled backdoor injection and removal.

Conclusion: The study opens a new direction for LLM safety, where defenders can leverage cross-backdoor transfer to suppress unknown backdoors by deliberately injecting and removing controlled backdoors.

Abstract: Backdoor attacks in Large Language Models (LLMs) are a growing security concern, where models can generate adversary-chosen content. Existing defenses target backdoors one at a time and typically require knowledge of the trigger, leaving the defender at a structural disadvantage when unknown backdoors may exist in a model. We show that backdoor neutralization through unlearning generalizes across backdoors: training a model to ignore a single trigger can also suppress other backdoors that were never explicitly targeted. We study this phenomenon across three model families, whose backdoors were injected via pretraining or continual pretraining, by analyzing the models obtained after removing one backdoor at a time. To understand why unlearning certain backdoors induces the suppression of others, we introduce the Cross Activation Shift Distance, to quantify the distance between model changes induced by different trainings. Our results open a new direction for LLM safety as defenders could deliberately inject controlled backdoors and then remove them, leveraging cross-backdoor transfer to also suppress unknown backdoors that an attacker may have previously introduced in the model.

</details>


### [202] [Exploring Adversarial Robustness and Safety Alignment in Multilingual Multi-Modal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03793)
*Hashmat Shadab Malik,Muzammal Naseer,Salman Khan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper studies the adversarial robustness and multimodal safety of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) across 12 languages, revealing vulnerabilities and variations in safety across languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the gap in understanding the multilingual behavior of MLLMs, specifically their robustness and safety across diverse languages.

Method: The method involves evaluating open-source MLLMs using gradient-based attacks and analyzing their performance across 12 languages.

Result: The results show that MLLMs exhibit transferable multilingual vulnerability, with adversarial images optimized in one language inducing failures in others. The safety of MLLMs varies across languages, with languages with stronger linguistic grounding more often eliciting misuse-enabling responses.

Conclusion: The conclusion is that deeper integration of multilingual capability across training stages leads to genuine multilingual safety alignment, whereas shallow multilingual adaptation may produce illusory safety in low-resource languages.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models integrate visual perception into language reasoning, introducing a continuous attack surface susceptible to adversarial attacks. Prior work on MLLM robustness has focused largely on English-centric tasks, leaving multilingual behaviour unexplored. We address this gap through a systematic study of adversarial robustness and multimodal safety across 12 diverse languages, evaluating open-source MLLMs that acquire multilingual capability through instruction tuning. Gradient-based attacks reveal a transferable multilingual vulnerability: adversarial images optimized in one language continue to induce failure in others, demonstrating strong cross-lingual transferability. Multilingual safety further varies with how effectively a model retrieves or interprets harmful instructions. When harmful intent is issued through text, languages with stronger linguistic grounding more often elicit misuse-enabling responses, while weaker languages produce fewer unsafe outputs. When embedded in the image as typographic content, English scripts are reliably recognised and followed, whereas non-English scripts are rarely parsed by the vision encoder. Lower-resource languages may therefore appear safer, but this is an artefact of comprehension and visual-grounding failures rather than genuine alignment, a phenomenon we term safety-by-failure. In contrast, MLLMs that build multilingual capability throughout their training stages rather than only at instruction tuning, such as Qwen3-VL, exhibit genuine cross-lingual safety, maintaining active refusal across languages rather than masking comprehension failure. Shallow multilingual adaptation, such as fine-tuning on translated instruction data, may produce surface-level understanding that creates illusory safety in low-resource languages; deeper integration across training stages leads to genuine multilingual safety alignment.

</details>


### [203] [Consistency Training Can Entrench Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03810)
*David Demitri Africa,Arathi Mani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Consistency training methods can have varying effects on model alignment, sometimes suppressing and other times amplifying misaligned behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation behind this paper is to investigate the impact of consistency training methods on model alignment, specifically whether these methods can amplify undesired behavior in models.

Method: The authors test seven consistency training methods on 108 open-source models (7B-70B) fine-tuned to exhibit controlled misaligned behavior.

Result: The study finds that consistency training generally suppresses reward hacking and emergent misalignment but amplifies sycophancy, with distribution shifts induced by the consistency labeling process being a primary driver of systematic alignment effects.

Conclusion: The study concludes that consistency training is not alignment-neutral and that its use in critical systems should be carefully audited.

Abstract: Consistency training encourages a model to produce similar outputs across related inputs or sampling procedures. Such methods are simple, scalable, and largely label-free, but their effects on model alignment remain poorly understood. Could the self-bootstrapping nature of these methods amplify undesired behavior in models? We test seven consistency training methods on 108 ``model organisms: open-source models (7B--70B) fine-tuned to exhibit various forms of controlled misaligned behavior. We find that outcomes vary significantly: consistency training generally suppresses reward hacking and emergent misalignment but amplifies sycophancy. We present evidence that distribution shifts induced by the consistency labeling process, rather than variation in the selection operators, may be the primary driver of systematic alignment effects. Finally, we present a unifying theoretical framework to derive conditions under which consistency training will amplify or suppress misalignment. In total, our study establishes that consistency training is not alignment-neutral, and that its use in critical systems should be carefully audited.

</details>


### [204] [Rethinking the Idiomaticity Decomposability Hypothesis: Evidence from Distributional Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03817)
*Maggie Mi,Golzar Atefi,Atsuki Yamaguchi,Felix Gers,Aline Villavicencio,Nafise Sadat Moosavi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper examines the decomposability of idioms and its relation to syntactic flexibility and predictability using contextualized language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the relationship between idiom decomposability, syntactic flexibility, and distributional experience, and to understand how idiom representations are learned during pretraining.

Method: Using contextualized language models as controlled distributional learners, the authors propose a model-internal measure of decomposability and relate it to human ratings, syntactic flexibility, and predictability.

Result: The results show that model-derived decomposability correlates weakly with human judgments and has a small but consistent negative relationship with syntactic flexibility. Additionally, decomposability, surprisal, and frequency all contribute to the stabilization of idiom representations in models during pretraining.

Conclusion: The study suggests that idiom behavior is influenced by distributional experience, and that decomposability plays a significant role in shaping idiom representations during pretraining.

Abstract: Idioms can be analysed in terms of their decomposability, the extent to which constituent meanings contribute to the figurative whole. Decomposability is thought to predict syntactic flexibility. Usage-based accounts instead attribute idiom behaviour to distributional experience, such as speaker familiarity and predictability. We examine these views using contextualised language models as controlled distributional learners. We propose a model-internal measure of decomposability and relate it to human ratings, syntactic flexibility, and predictability while tracking idiom learning during pretraining. Model-derived decomposability correlates weakly with human judgments and shows a small but consistent negative relationship with syntactic flexibility. Pretraining analyses show that stabilisation of idiom representations in models is not explained by frequency alone. Instead, surprisal, decomposability, and frequency all contribute, with decomposability showing the strongest training-dependent effect.

</details>


### [205] [Clustered Self-Assessment: A Simple yet Effective Method for Uncertainty Quantification in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03846)
*Qi Cao,Takeshi Kojima,Andrew Gambardella,Helinyi Peng,Yutaka Matsuo,Yusuke Iwasawa*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a simple and effective method for uncertainty quantification in large language models (LLMs) by leveraging the model's self-assessment capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often generate factually incorrect responses and lack explicit uncertainty estimates, making it difficult for users to judge the reliability of model outputs.

Method: The proposed method groups sampled generations into semantically distinct clusters, converts them into answer options in a structured multiple-choice question, and uses the probability assigned by the LLM to each option as a confidence estimate.

Result: The method consistently outperforms baseline approaches across multiple models and datasets, achieving competitive performance with as few as two additional samples.

Conclusion: The proposed self-assessment method provides a simple yet effective approach for uncertainty quantification in LLMs, demonstrating both effectiveness and efficiency.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate remarkable performance across diverse tasks, but they often generate responses that appear plausible while being factually incorrect. This problem is compounded by the lack of explicit uncertainty estimates, which makes it difficult for users to judge the reliability of model outputs. Existing uncertainty quantification methods typically rely on indirect signals, such as entropy across sampled generations. These signals can be difficult to interpret and do not fully leverage the model's ability to assess its own uncertainty. We propose a simple yet effective self-assessment method for uncertainty quantification in LLMs. Our approach groups sampled generations into semantically distinct clusters, converts them into answer options in a structured multiple-choice question, and uses the probability assigned by the LLM to each option as a confidence estimate. Experiments across multiple models and datasets show that our method consistently outperforms baseline approaches. Notably, it achieves competitive performance with as few as two additional samples, demonstrating both its effectiveness and efficiency.

</details>


### [206] [A Training-Free Mixture-of-Agents Framework for Multi-Document Summarization using LLMs and Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03867)
*Cuong Vuong Tuan,Trang Mai Xuan,Tien-Cuong Nguyen,Vu-Duc Ngo,Thien Van Luong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A new framework for multi-document summarization that combines large language models and knowledge graphs without requiring labeled training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches to multi-document summarization struggle with complex inter-document relationships, require large amounts of labeled data, and have limited generalization across domains and languages.

Method: A mixture-of-agents framework that decomposes summarization into specialized tasks: extractive selection, knowledge-aware abstraction, and iterative refinement, unified by a multi-perspective consistency mechanism.

Result: State-of-the-art or competitive performance on four datasets in English and Vietnamese.

Conclusion: The proposed framework is effective and adaptable for multi-document summarization across domains and languages.

Abstract: Multi-Document Summarization (MDS) plays a critical role in distilling essential information from collections of textual data. Existing approaches often struggle to capture complex inter-document relationships, rely heavily on large amounts of labeled data for supervised training, or exhibit limited generalization across domains and languages. To address these limitations, we present a training-free mixture-of-agents framework for MDS that leverages the complementary strengths of large language models (LLMs) and knowledge graphs. Our approach decomposes summarization into specialized agent tasks: extractive selection, knowledge-aware abstraction, and iterative refinement, each operating without task-specific fine-tuning. We unify their outputs using a multi-perspective consistency mechanism guided by LLMs. Experiments across four datasets in English and Vietnamese demonstrate state-of-the-art or competitive performance, validating the effectiveness and adaptability of our modular design.

</details>


### [207] [RealClawBench: Live OpenClaw Benchmarks from Real Developer-Agent Sessions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03889)
*Zongwei Lv,Zhewen Tan,Yaoming Li,Yilun Yao,Yuxuan Tian,Lin Sun,Xiangzheng Zhang,Weihong Lin,Tong Yang,Guangxiang Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduced RealClawBench, a live benchmark framework for evaluating agent performance in real-world scenarios, capturing distribution, diversity, and difficulty of deployed agent use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks lack realism in reflecting actual user requests and deployed agent use, making it essential to create a more realistic evaluation framework.
